E.H. is supported in part by the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) under award number DE-SC0009937, and by the Mani L. Bhaumik Institute for Theoretical Physics. M.K. is supported by the Yale Mossman Prize Postdoctoral Fellowship.

Appendix A
Notation, conventions, and coordinate systems

Our metric conventions are mostly-minus,
\{+,-,\dots,-\}
. We use
x,z
with indices
\mu,\nu
to refer to
d
dimensional vectors. Unless otherwise noted,
d
dimensional index contractions are performed with the flat metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
. We also use
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{n}
to refer to
d-1
dimensional spatial vectors, and
\vec{n}^{\perp},\vec{y}^{\perp}
to refer to
d-2
dimensional transverse vectors.

In this appendix, we review several coordinate systems used for calculations involving detector operators. As nicely explained in e.g.
[
198
]
, instead of the time and radial coordinates used to write the energy detector in Eq. (
1
), it is convenient to adapt a more suitable coordinate parametrization. As we imagine the state formed either by a local excitation or a scattering event centered around
t=0
with the characteristic length scale
\sigma
, in theories with massless particles, such states spread asymptotically at the speed of light. This implies that after some time
t
, the excitation will be localized around some radius
r\sim t\pm\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
. From here, we see that the main contribution to the flux integrals in Eq. (
1
) comes from the integration region where
t\sim r\pm\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
approaches infinity. Therefore,
radial light-cone coordinates

r^{\pm}=t\pm r,

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}x^{0}\\
r\,\vec{n}^{\perp\phantom{d1}}\\
r\,n^{d-1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}+r^{-})\phantom{n^{d-1}}\\
\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}-r^{-})\vec{n}^{\perp\phantom{d1}}\\
\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}-r^{-})n^{d-1}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(142)

provide a more suitable parametrization of the relevant physics. For later convenience, we split the unit vector
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}
into sub-components
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}=(\vec{n}^{\perp},n^{d-1})
,
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{2}=1\Rightarrow 1=(\vec{n}^{\perp})^{2}+(n^{d-1})^{2}
. For any value of
r^{+}
, the state is localized around a finite value of
r^{-}\sim\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
. In these coordinates, the Minkowski metric is given by:

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\eta_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}=dr^{-}dr^{+}-\frac{1}{4}(r^{+}-r^{-})^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}\,,

(143)

where
d\Omega^{2}_{d-2}
denotes the square of the line element on the
(d-2)
-dimensional celestial sphere. Note that
r^{\pm}
are null coordinates that satisfy
\eta^{\mu\nu}(\nabla_{\mu}r^{\pm})(\nabla_{\nu}r^{\pm})=0
. In conformal theories, one can show that not only the
total
charge, but also the time integrated flux is independent of the choice of surface if it approaches future null infinity
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. In the radial light-cone coordinates, the charge and energy detector read

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\widehat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}dr^{-}\ \lim_{r^{+}\to\infty}\left(\frac{r^{+}}{2}\right)^{d-2}J^{\,r^{+}}(r^{+},r^{-},\widehat{\mathbf{n}})\,,

(144)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}dr^{-}\ \lim_{r^{+}\to\infty}\left(\frac{r^{+}}{2}\right)^{d-2}T^{\,r^{+}r^{+}}(r^{+},r^{-},\widehat{\mathbf{n}})\,.

(145)

It is important to stress that for external non-localized states, like momentum eigenstates, we need to take the large
r^{+}
limit first and then perform the
r^{-}
integral. The opposite is usually done in CFT for localized states, for which all notions are equivalent
[
44
,
92
]
.
Despite their more convenient description of the asymptotic region
r^{+}\to\infty
,
r^{-},\widehat{\mathbf{n}}
, finite, the radial light-cone coordinates lead to the nontrivial Minkioski metric in Eq. (
143
).

If one is allowed to perform a conformal transformation
x^{\mu}\to y^{\mu}(x)
, one can set up coordinates that retain the advantageous of radial light-cone coordinates, but keep the metric simple. Starting from
ordinary light-cone coordinates
defined via
x^{\pm}=x^{0}\pm x^{d-1}
and
\vec{x}^{\perp}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{d-2})
, (with
t\equiv x^{0}
) where the Minkowski metric reads

\displaystyle ds^{2}=dx^{-}dx^{+}-d\vec{x}^{\perp}\cdot d\vec{x}^{\perp}\,,

(146)

one can perform the conformal mapping
[
44
]

\displaystyle y^{+}=-\frac{1}{x^{+}}\,,\quad y^{-}=x^{-}-\frac{|\vec{x}^{\perp}|^{2}}{x^{+}}\,,\quad\vec{y}^{\perp}=\frac{\vec{x}^{\perp}}{x^{+}}\,,

(147)

to the
y
-light-cone variables that are defined in analogy to
x
. With this conformal transformation, the metric in Eq. (
146
) changes by a conformal factor

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{1}{(y^{+})^{2}}\left(dy^{-}dy^{+}-d\vec{y}^{\perp}\cdot d\vec{y}^{\perp}\right)

(148)

and
\mathscr{I}^{+}
gets mapped to the null plane
y^{+}=0
. Parametrizing
x^{\mu}
via the radial light-cone coordinates introduced in Eq. (
142
) maps the surface where
r^{+}=\infty
(keeping
r^{-},\hat{n}
fixed) to the plane
y^{+}=0
. On this plane,

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{+}&=x^{0}+x^{d-1}=\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}+r^{-})+\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}-r^{-})n^{d-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{+}\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{r^{+}}{2}(1+n^{d-1})\\
\vec{x}^{\perp}&=\frac{1}{2}(r^{+}-r^{-})\vec{n}^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{+}\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{1}{2}r^{+}\vec{n}^{\perp}\end{split}

(149)

one can show that
y^{-}
and
\vec{y}^{\perp}
are finite functions of
r^{-}
and
\hat{n}

\displaystyle y^{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{+}\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{2r^{-}}{1+n^{d-1}}\,,\quad\vec{y}^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r^{+}\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{\vec{n}^{\perp}}{1+n^{d-1}}\,,

(150)

where we used the fact that
\hat{n}
is normalized
\hat{n}^{2}=1=\left(\vec{n}^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\left(\vec{n}^{d-1}\right)^{2}
. Furthermore, the null line defined by
r^{+}=\infty,\,\hat{n}=\text{fixed}
maps to the null line defined by
y^{+}=0,\,\vec{y}^{\perp}=\text{fixed}
. In the limit where
t\approx r
, i.e.
y^{+}\to 0^{-}
, the various coordinate systems are related by

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{-y^{+}}+y^{-}\right)\,,\quad

\displaystyle r^{\phantom{-}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{-y^{+}}-\left[\frac{1-|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}\right]y^{-}\right)\,,

(151)

\displaystyle r^{+}

\displaystyle=t+r=\frac{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{-y^{+}}-\frac{|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}y^{-}\,,\quad

\displaystyle r^{-}=t-r=\frac{y^{-}}{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}\,,

(152)

and the perpendicular directions are related via
\vec{n}^{\perp}=\left(\frac{2}{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}\right)\vec{y}^{\perp}
, so that the spherical measure becomes

\displaystyle d\Omega_{d-2}=\left(\frac{2}{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}\right)^{d-2}d\vec{y}^{\perp}\,.

(153)

In the conformal coordinate system, the energy flux operators are related to light-ray operators by an integral over the null line

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\vec{y}^{\perp})=\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}dy^{-}\,J_{-}(y^{+}=0,y^{-},\vec{y}^{\perp})\,,\quad\mathcal{E}(\vec{y}^{\perp})=2\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}dy^{-}\,T_{--}(y^{+}=0,y^{-},\vec{y}^{\perp})\,.

(154)

The relation to the original flux operators is obtained by taking into account the transformation of the measure and the currents

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\hat{n})=\left(\frac{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d-2}\mathcal{Q}(\vec{y}^{\perp})\,,\quad\mathcal{E}(\hat{n})=\left(\frac{1+|\vec{y}^{\perp}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d-1}\mathcal{E}(\vec{y}^{\perp})\,.

(155)

A detailed discussion of these coordinate systems can be found in Appendix A of
[
198
]
.

Note that Ref.
[
33
]
starts from light-cone coordinates

\displaystyle ds^{2}=dx^{-}dx^{+}-dx^{1}dx^{1}-dx^{2}dx^{2}\,,

(156)

where
x^{\pm}=x^{0}\pm x^{3}
and subsequently changes variables to
flat null coordinates

\displaystyle ds^{2}=du\,dr-r^{2}dzd\overline{z}\,.

(157)

In these coordinates, the null boundaries are located at
r\to\infty
for
(u,z,\overline{z})
held fixed, and
z,\overline{z}
are stereographic coordinates on the celestial sphere. The two sets of coordinates in Eqs. (
156
) and (
157
) are related via the transformation

\displaystyle x^{+}=r\,,\quad x^{-}=u+r\,z\overline{z}\,,\quad x^{1}=\frac{1}{2}r(z+\overline{z})\,,\quad x^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}i\,r(z-\overline{z})\,.

(158)

Converting back to the standard coordinates, gives the following relations:

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=\left(\frac{r}{2}\left(1+z\overline{z}+\frac{u}{r}\right),\frac{1}{2}r(z+\overline{z}),-\frac{1}{2}i\,r(z-\overline{z}),\frac{r}{2}\left(1-z\overline{z}-\frac{u}{r}\right)\right)\,.

(159)

In the gravitational context, Ref.
[
33
]
makes use of yet another set of coordinates which are tailored for the asymptotic large
r
expansion near
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In
d=4
, the
Bondi coordinates
are given by

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=\left(v+r,r\frac{w+\overline{w}}{1+w\overline{w}},-i\,r\frac{w-\overline{w}}{1+w\overline{w}},r\frac{1-w\overline{w}}{1+w\overline{w}}\right)\,,

(160)

where the retarded time is
v=t-r
. The Minkowski metric in these coordinates is

\displaystyle ds^{2}=dv^{2}+2dv\,dr-2r^{2}\gamma_{w\overline{w}}dwd\overline{w}\,,

(161)

where the metric on the celestial sphere is given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{w\overline{w}}=\frac{2}{(1+w\overline{w})^{2}}\,.

(162)

Asymptotically flat geometries
that admit a large
r
asymptic expansions

\displaystyle ds^{2}

\displaystyle=dv^{2}+2dv\,dr-2r^{2}\gamma_{w\overline{w}}dwd\overline{w}\ +

(163)

\displaystyle\Bigg\{

\displaystyle-\frac{2m_{B}}{r}dv^{2}-rC_{ww}dw^{2}-D^{w}C_{ww}dvdw-\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{4}{3}N_{w}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{w}\left(C_{ww}C^{ww}\right)\right]dvdw+\left(w\leftrightarrow\overline{w}\right)+\cdots\Bigg\}

beyond the leading order Minkowski spacetime are called Christodoulou-Klainerman spaces. Here one introduces the Bondi mass aspect
m_{B}(v,w,\overline{w})
, the shear tensor
C_{\alpha\beta}(v,w,\overline{w})
, and the Bondi angular momentum aspect
(N_{w}(v,w,\overline{w}),\,N_{\overline{w}}(v,w,\overline{w}))
.
D^{w}
is the covariant derivative on the celestial sphere.

Appendix B
Detectors in Bondi gauge

For a review of different coordinates used for taking limits in asymptotically flat spacetimes, see App.
A
. To construct the detector operators, it is convenient to use Bondi coordinates (see Eq. (
160
)),

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=(v+r,r\widehat{\mathbf{n}})

(164)

The coordinate
v
is the retarded time, and
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}
is a
d-1
dimensional unit vector parametrizing the celestial sphere. In
d=4
in Bondi coordinates, one uses Poincaré coordinates
w,\overline{w}
on the celestial
S^{2}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}=\left(\frac{w+\overline{w}}{1+w\overline{w}},-i\frac{\left(w-\overline{w}\right)}{1+w\overline{w}},\frac{1-w\overline{w}}{1+w\overline{w}}\right)\,.

(165)

The essential object is the shear tensor (see Eq. (
163
)), which in Bondi coordinates is related to the leading
1/r
term of the gravitational field via

\displaystyle C_{ww}(v,w,\overline{w})=-\kappa\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}h_{ww}(r,v,w,\overline{w})\,.

(166)

The graviton energy detector in
d=4
then takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{h}(\widehat{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dv(\partial_{v}C_{ww})(\partial_{v}C^{ww})

\displaystyle=2\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}dv\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\gamma^{w\overline{w}})^{2}\left(\partial_{v}h_{ww}\right)\left(\partial_{v}h_{\overline{w}\overline{w}}\right)\,.

(167)

This agrees with our expression Eq. (
46
) in Bondi gauge.
The energy detector acts on asymptotic graviton states
|X\rangle=|\{p_{1},\sigma_{1}\},\cdots,\{p_{n},\sigma_{n}\}\rangle
as in Eq. (
4
), namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\widehat{\mathbf{n}})|X\rangle=\sum^{m}_{i=1}E_{i}\,\delta^{d-2}(\Omega_{\widehat{\mathbf{k}}_{i}}-\Omega_{\widehat{\mathbf{n}}})|X\rangle\,.

(168)

Ref.
[
33
]
used this definition to compute the two-point function of energy flux operators in the classical limit.

It is straightforward to generalize the definition (
167
) to the Regge trajectory of
E^{J-1}
detectors, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{J_{L}}(z)\propto\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}\,\psi_{J_{L}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):C_{ww}(\alpha_{1},z)C^{ww}(\alpha_{2},z):\,.

(169)

Once again, this agrees with the definition Eq. (
53
) in Bondi gauge.

Appendix C
Graviton detector vertex

In this appendix we compute the detector vertex

\displaystyle\langle 0|h_{\mu\nu}(-q)\mathcal{D}_{J_{L}}(z)h_{\rho\sigma}(p)|0\rangle\,.

(170)

The analogous detector vertex for scalar field theory was computed in
[
80
]
, we proceed similarly. Plugging in for the detector (
53
) and Wick contracting, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{J_{L}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\alpha_{1}d\alpha_{2}\,\psi_{J_{L}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\langle 0|h_{\mu\nu}(-q)h_{\alpha\beta}(\alpha_{1},z)|0\rangle\langle 0|h^{\alpha\beta}(\alpha_{2},z)h_{\rho\sigma}(p)|0\rangle+(\alpha_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{2}).

(171)

Noting that

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}(p)=\hat{\delta}(p^{2})\sum_{s}\left[\theta(-p^{0})\varepsilon^{s,*}_{\mu\nu}(p)a_{s}(p)+\theta(p^{0})\varepsilon^{s}_{\mu\nu}(p)a_{s}^{\dagger}(p)\right],

(172)

we can compute

\displaystyle\langle 0|h_{\mu\nu}(\alpha,z)h_{\rho\sigma}(p)|0\rangle=\frac{ie^{-i\frac{d\pi}{4}}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(p)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}d\beta\beta^{\Delta_{h}-1}e^{-i\frac{\beta\alpha}{2}}\delta^{d}(p-\beta z).

(173)

With this, we can compute the detector vertex by evaluating the integrals over
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, obtaining (
62
).

Appendix D
Detector kinematics

In this appendix we provide a review of the different coordinates used to parameterize our detectors. We frequently use null vectors
z^{\mu}
to parametrize the angular position of detectors. These null vectors are naturally polarization vectors for underlying light-ray operators. They satisfy the following conditions

\displaystyle z^{2}=0,\qquad z\sim\lambda z\qquad(\lambda>0).

(174)

These redundancies can be solved in particular coordinates systems. Two common parametrizations of the null vectors are in the center of mass frame,

\displaystyle z=(z^{0},\mathbf{z})=(1,\widehat{\mathbf{n}}),\qquad\text{with }\quad\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{2}=1,

(175)

which we will discuss in detail below, or in terms of a null plane,

\displaystyle z=(x^{+},x^{-},\vec{x}^{\perp})=(1,y^{2},\vec{y}^{\perp})\,.

(176)

in light-cone variables with
y^{2}=\vec{y}^{\perp}\cdot\vec{y}^{\perp}
.
We define and frequently use the following cross ratios

\displaystyle\zeta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{(z_{a}\cdot z_{b})(P\cdot P)}{2(P\cdot z_{a})(P\cdot z_{b})}\,,

(177)

\displaystyle\chi_{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(Q\cdot z_{a})}{(P\cdot z_{a})}\,,

(178)

where
P^{\mu}=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{\mu}
, and
Q^{\mu}=(p_{1}-p_{2})^{\mu}
.

Center of mass frame.

It is convenient to define the center of mass frame for the two particle incoming state. In standard Minkowski coordinates
(x^{0},x^{1},\dots,x^{d-1})
we have

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}(1,x\,\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{0})\,,\qquad p_{2}^{\mu}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}(1,-x\,\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{0})\,,

(179)

where
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{0}
is the unit vector characterizing the beam direction, and
x=\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}/\sqrt{s}
is a dimensionless variable. This implies

\displaystyle P^{\mu}=\sqrt{s}(1,\mathbf{0})\,,\qquad Q^{\mu}=\sqrt{s}(0,x\,\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{0})\,.

(180)

In the center of mass frame, it is convenient to choose the detector positions as

\displaystyle z_{a}=(1,\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{a})\,,

(181)

where
\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{a}
are unit
d-1
vectors parametrizing points on the
(d-2)
-dimensional celestial sphere. This has the physical interpretation that
\mathbf{\hat{n}}_{a}
are the angles of the detectors on the celestial sphere. For the following, we pick
d=4
for concreteness.

There are two sets of coordinates on the celestial sphere that are particularly useful. We can parametrize points on the celestial sphere using Poincaré coordinates,

\displaystyle z_{a}=(1,\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{a}),\quad\text{with}\quad\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{a}=\left(\frac{\overline{w}_{a}+w_{a}}{1+w_{a}\overline{w}_{a}},-\frac{i\left(w_{a}-\overline{w}_{a}\right)}{1+w_{a}\overline{w}_{a}},\frac{1-w_{a}\overline{w}_{a}}{1+w_{a}\overline{w}_{a}}\right)\,,

(182)

where
w_{a},\overline{w}_{a}\in\mathbb{C}
are complex conjugates. Alternatively, we can simply use angular coordinates:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{a}=(\sin\theta_{a}\cos\phi_{a},\sin\theta_{a}\sin\phi_{a},\cos\theta_{a})\,.

(183)

For our purposes, it will be convenient to use a combination of both sets of coordinates.

Let us first determine the relative coordinates of the detector system and take into account the relative rotation with respect to the beam axis
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{0}
afterwards. For the system of detectors we will use Poincaré coordinates. In terms of the complex conjugate parameters
(w_{a},\overline{w}_{a})
, we can express the cross-ratios in Eq. (
177
) as

\displaystyle\zeta_{ab}=\frac{\left(w_{a}-w_{b}\right)\left(\overline{w}_{a}-\overline{w}_{b}\right)}{\left(1+w_{a}\overline{w}_{a}\right)\left(1+w_{b}\overline{w}_{b}\right)}\,.

(184)

Using translations on the celestial sphere, we choose
w_{1}=\overline{w}_{1}=0
for the relative position of the first detector, and
w_{2}=\overline{w}_{2}=\sqrt{\zeta}/\sqrt{1-\zeta}
for the second detector. This choice ensures that
\zeta_{12}=\zeta
. Adding a third detector,

\displaystyle w_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{1-\zeta}}z\,,\qquad\overline{w}_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{1-\zeta}}\overline{z}\,.

(185)

we rescaled
(w_{3},\overline{w}_{3})
in order to have a single overall detector separation variable
\zeta
that can be used to access e.g. the multi-collinear limit.
16
16

16

We hope it is clear from the context that the celestial sphere coordinates
z,\overline{z}
are
not
to be confused with the four-vectors defined around Eq. (
9
).
In summary, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{1}

\displaystyle=(0,0,1),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{2}

\displaystyle=(2\sqrt{\zeta(1-\zeta)},0,1-2\zeta),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{\sqrt{1-\zeta}\sqrt{\zeta}(z+\overline{z})}{1-\zeta\left(1-z\overline{z}\right)},i\tfrac{\sqrt{1-\zeta}\sqrt{\zeta}\left(z-\overline{z}\right)}{1-\zeta\left(1-z\overline{z}\right)},\tfrac{1-\zeta\left(1+z\overline{z}\right)}{1-\zeta\left(1-z\overline{z}\right)}\right).

(186)

In these variables, the three detector system is parametrized by
\zeta,z,\overline{z}
, with

\displaystyle\zeta_{12}=\zeta,\qquad\zeta_{23}=\frac{(1-z)(1-\overline{z})}{1-(1-z\overline{z})\zeta}\zeta(1-\zeta),\qquad\zeta_{31}=\frac{z\overline{z}}{1-(1-z\overline{z})\zeta}\zeta\,.

(187)

In particular, the triple-collinear limit is taken as
\zeta\to 0
, with

\displaystyle\zeta_{12}=\zeta,\qquad\zeta_{23}=(1-z)(1-\overline{z})\zeta+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{2}),\qquad\zeta_{31}=z\overline{z}\zeta+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{2})\,.

(188)

It is also useful to define the angle between detectors 1 and 2 as
\cos\theta=\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{2}
, so that

\displaystyle\zeta=\frac{1-\cos\theta}{2}=\sin^{2}(\theta/2)\,.

(189)

Having specified the relative coordinates of our detectors, we are left with orienting the entire detector system relative to the beam axis
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{0}
. We can do this by rotating the beam axis by angles
(\psi,\phi)
relative to the first detector:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{0}=R_{xy}(-\phi)\cdot R_{zx}(-\psi)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=\left(-\sin\psi\cos\phi,\sin\psi\sin\phi,\cos\psi\right)\,.

(190)

Alternatively, we can fix the beam axis to
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathbf{\hat{z}}
, and rotate the system of detectors

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=(0,0,1)\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=R_{zx}(\psi)\cdot R_{xy}(\phi)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{1}=R_{zx}(\psi)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=R_{zx}(\psi)\cdot R_{xy}(\phi)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=R_{zx}(\psi)\cdot R_{xy}(-\phi)\cdot R_{zx}(\theta)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=R_{zx}(\psi)\cdot R_{xy}(\phi)\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{n}}_{3}\,

(191)

Clearly, the
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}
systems are related by an overall rotation of the celestial sphere and are equivalent. The three-detector system together with the two-particle initial state is therefore characterized by five variables:
\phi,\psi
, and
\zeta,z,\overline{z}
.

Having discussed the beam axis, we can now evaluate the remaining cross ratios
\chi_{a}
of Eq. (
177
)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}

\displaystyle=x\cos\psi\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{2}

\displaystyle=x\left((1-2\zeta)\cos\psi-2\sqrt{1-\zeta}\sqrt{\zeta}\sin\psi\cos\phi\right)\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{3}

\displaystyle=x\left(\cos\psi-\frac{\sqrt{1-\zeta}\sqrt{\zeta}\sin\psi\left(\left(z+\overline{z}\right)\cos\phi+i\left(z-\overline{z}\right)\sin\phi\right)}{1-\zeta\left(1-z\overline{z}\right)}\right)\,.

(192)

Alternatively, we can trade
\zeta
for
\theta
, using Eq. (
189
) and the
relation
2\sqrt{1-\zeta}\sqrt{\zeta}=\sin\theta
.

Appendix E
Higher-point correlators

Similar to the discussion of the 2-point correlator in Sec.
3.2.2
, we can determine the leading finite angle contribution to a
n
-point correlator, involving amplitudes with
m=n+1
outgoing legs. For three-particle correlators we can set
n=3
at the end as necessary. Starting from the general detector formula in Eq. (
73
) and focusing on the first correction due to the additional exchange of a massless state, we discuss

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{n}=\vbox{\hbox{

}}\,.

(193)

Repeating the steps above, we immediately have the following integral over the square of the underlying
k\to n+1
amplitude;

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{n}(p_{i},z_{a})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{k}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\hat{d}^{d}q_{i}\prod_{a=1}^{n}V_{J_{La}}(q_{a},z_{a})\hat{\delta}^{+}(q_{n+1}^{2})\hat{\delta}^{d}(P-\sum^{n+1}_{j=1}q_{j})\big|\mathcal{M}_{k\to n+1}(p_{i},q_{j})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{k}\,(2\pi)^{nd}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod^{n}_{a=1}d\beta_{a}\,\beta_{a}^{-J_{La}-1}\,\hat{\delta}^{+}((P-\sum^{n}_{a=1}\beta_{a}z_{a})^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left|\mathcal{M}_{k\to n+1}\left(p_{i},\,q_{a}{=}\beta_{a}z_{a},\,q_{n+1}{=}P{-}\sum_{a}\beta_{a}z_{a}\right)\right|^{2}\,.

(194)

Changing to
\alpha_{a}
variables, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{n}(p_{i},z_{a})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{k}\,(2\pi)^{nd-1}}\frac{1}{P\cdot P}\prod^{n}_{a=1}\left[\frac{2P\cdot z_{a}}{P\cdot P}\right]^{J_{La}}

(195)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod^{n}_{a=1}d\alpha_{a}\,\alpha_{a}^{-J_{La}-1}\,\theta(2{-}\sum^{n}_{a=1}\alpha_{a})\,\delta\left(1{-}\sum^{n}_{a=1}\alpha_{a}{+}\sum^{n}_{a<b}\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}\,\zeta_{ab}\right)\big|\mathcal{M}_{k\to n+1}(p_{i},q_{j}^{**})\big|^{2}\,.

The amplitude squared is evaluated at the momenta

\displaystyle q_{a}^{**}

\displaystyle=\frac{P\cdot P}{2P\cdot z_{a}}\alpha_{a}z_{a}\,,\qquad\qquad q_{n+1}^{**}=P-\sum_{a=1}^{n}q_{a}^{**}\,.

(196)

Performing one of the
\alpha_{a}
integrals, say
\alpha_{n}
, using the delta function fixes

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}=\alpha_{n}^{*}(\alpha_{a})\equiv\frac{1-\!\!\sum\limits_{1\leq a<n}\!\!\alpha_{a}+\!\!\!\!\sum\limits_{1\leq a<b<n}\!\!\!\!\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}\,\zeta_{ab}}{1-\!\!\sum\limits_{1\leq a<n}\!\!\alpha_{a}\,\zeta_{an}}.

(197)

These integrals are in general much more complicated than in the collinear limit, as discussed in Sec.
3.3
. The three-point energy correlator for generic kinematics has been computed in both QCD and
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
[
52
,
53
]
.

Appendix F
Expressions for the DDC in the collinear limit

In section
5.4
, we computed the collinear limit of detector-detector correlators (DDC) of
\mathcal{D}_{J_{L}}
detector. Here we present the functions
h_{2}(y)
and
h_{0}(y)
that appear in the result (
140
),

\displaystyle h_{2}(y)=-\frac{(1+y)}{2y^{3}}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-2\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-4\right)}\left(y^{4}+4y^{3}+6y^{2}+4y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-1\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-3\right)}2\left(2y^{4}+7y^{3}+9y^{2}+5y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}\left(14y^{4}+42y^{3}+45y^{2}+20y+6\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(1-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-1\right)}2\left(14y^{4}+35y^{3}+29y^{2}+8y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(2-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}\right)}\left(35y^{4}+70y^{3}+37y^{2}-6y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(3-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+1\right)}2y\left(14y^{3}+21y^{2}+2y-14\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(4-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+2\right)}2y\left(7y^{3}+7y^{2}-4y-14\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(5-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+3\right)}4(y-2)y\left(y^{2}+2y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(6-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+4\right)}(y-2)y\left(y^{2}+2y+2\right)\Bigg]\,,

(198)

and

\displaystyle h_{0}(y)=\frac{1}{4y^{4}}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-2\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-4\right)}\left(2y^{2}+2y+1\right)\left(y^{4}+4y^{3}+6y^{2}+4y+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-1\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-3\right)}4\left(2y^{5}+9y^{4}+16y^{3}+14y^{2}+7y+1\right)y

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}14\left(2y^{4}+8y^{3}+12y^{2}+8y+3\right)y^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(1-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}-1\right)}4\left(14y^{5}+49y^{4}+62y^{3}+31y^{2}+9y-2\right)y

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(2-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}\right)}\left(70y^{5}+210y^{4}+227y^{3}+100y^{2}+44y-4\right)y

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(3-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+1\right)}4\left(14y^{4}+35y^{3}+33y^{2}+17y+12\right)y^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(4-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+2\right)}2\left(14y^{4}+28y^{3}+29y^{2}+30y+22\right)y^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Gamma\left(5-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+3\right)}4\left(2y^{4}+2y^{3}+3y^{2}+8y+6\right)y^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\Gamma\left(6-J_{{L1}}\right)\Gamma\left(-J_{{L2}}-2\right)}{\Gamma\left(-J_{{L1}}-J_{{L2}}+4\right)}\left(2y^{4}+2y^{3}+3y^{2}+8y+6\right)y^{2}\Bigg]\,.

(199)
